=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a positive integer N and a length-N string s_0s_1\ldots s_{N-1} consisting only of 0 and 1.
Consider a simple undirected graph G with (N+1) vertices numbered 0, 1, 2, \ldots, N, and the following edges:

- For each i = 0, 1, \ldots, N-1, there is an undirected edge between vertices i and (i+1)\bmod N.
- For each i = 0, 1, \ldots, N-1, there is an undirected edge between vertices i and N if and only if s_i =  1.
- There are no other edges.

Furthermore, create a directed graph G' by assigning a direction to each edge of G. That is, for each undirected edge \lbrace u, v \rbrace in G, replace it with either a directed edge (u, v) from u to v or a directed edge (v, u) from v to u.
For each i = 0, 1, \ldots, N, let d_i be the in-degree of vertex i in G'. Print the number, modulo 998244353, of distinct sequences (d_0, d_1, \ldots, d_N) that can be obtained.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
s_0s_1\ldots s_{N-1}

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 3 \leq N \leq 10^6
- N is an integer.
- Each s_i is 0 or 1.

Sample Input 1

3
010

Sample Output 1

14

G has four undirected edges: \lbrace 0, 1 \rbrace, \lbrace 0, 2 \rbrace, \lbrace 1, 2 \rbrace, \lbrace 1, 3 \rbrace.
For example, if we assign directions to each edge as 0 \to 1, 2 \to 0, 2 \to 1, 1 \to 3, then (d_0, d_1, d_2, d_3) = (1, 2, 0, 1) is obtained.
The possible sequences (d_0, d_1, d_2, d_3) are
(0, 1, 2, 1), (0, 2, 1, 1), (0, 2, 2, 0), (0, 3, 1, 0), (1, 0, 2, 1), (1, 1, 1, 1), (1, 1, 2, 0), (1, 2, 0, 1), (1, 2, 1, 0), (1, 3, 0, 0), (2, 0, 1, 1), (2, 1, 0, 1), (2, 1, 1, 0), (2, 2, 0, 0), for a total of 14.

Sample Input 2

20
00001100111010100101

Sample Output 2

261339902

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Need check: If two different prefixes w1,w2 lead to same relation R, and we append a, the resulting strings w1+a and w2+a are distinct (because prefixes differ). But do we need count them separately? The DP over DFA states counts number of strings that reach each state, not number of equivalence classes? Let's recall: In a DFA, number of distinct strings of length t leading to state q can be counted by DP: newdp[δ(q,a)] += dp[q]. This counts each string exactly once because DFA is deterministic: each string maps to a unique state path; merging counts strings that end in same state but are distinct. dp[q] is count of distinct strings ending in q. When appending a, each such string gives a distinct longer string (since prefix distinct), so sum counts. If two different prefixes w1,w2 both in same q, appending same a yields different strings w1a,w2a, both lead to same q'; dp[q'] should include both. The recurrence dp[q'] += dp[q] does exactly that: it adds the number of strings in q, not just 1. So merging by relation is fine; dp values are counts of distinct strings, not number of equivalence classes. Good.

Function compose(R,T):
    res=0
    for p in range(2):
        for q in range(2):
            if R & (1 << ((p<<1)|q)):
                for r in range(2):
                    if T & (1 << ((q<<1)|r)):
                        res |= 1 << ((p<<1)|r)
    return res

Implementation plan:

```
import sys

MOD = 998244353

def compose(R, T):
    res = 0
    for u in range(2):
        for v in range(2):
            if R & (1 << ((u << 1) | v)):
                for w in range(2):
                    if T & (1 << ((v << 1) | w)):
                        res |= 1 << ((u << 1) | w)
    return res

def build_trans():
    T = [[0]*4 for _ in range(2)]
    # s=0
    T[0][0] = 1 << ((1 << 1) | 0)  # (1,0) bit2 -> 4
    T[0][1] = (1 << 0) | (1 << 3)  # 9
    T[0][2] = 1 << ((0 << 1) | 1)  # 2
    T[0][3] = 0
    # s=1
    T[1][0] = 4
    T[1][1] = (1 << 0) | (1 << 2) | (1 << 3)  # 13
    T[1][2] = (1 << 0) | (1 << 1) | (1 << 3)  # 11
    T[1][3] = 2
    trans = []
    for s in range(2):
        trans_s = [[] for _ in range(16)]
        for R in range(1,16):
            cnt = {}
            for a in range(4):
                R2 = compose(R, T[s][a])
                if R2:
                    cnt[R2] = cnt.get(R2, 0) + 1
            trans_s[R] = list(cnt.items())
        trans.append(trans_s)
    return trans
```

But T[0][1] = (1<<0)|(1<<3) =1+8=9. T[1][1] = (1<<0)|(1<<2)|(1<<3) =1+4+8=13. T[1][2] =1+2+8=11. Good.

Could precompute compose for all 16x16 maybe simpler:
```
comp = [[0]*16 for _ in range(16)]
for R in range(16):
  for T in range(16):
    ...
```
Then trans uses comp[R][T[s][a]].

Need ensure bit index formula: (u << 1) | v. For u=1,v=0 -> 2, bit 1<<2=4. Good.

Now DP:
```
def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    N = int(data[0])
    s = data[1].strip()
    trans = build_trans()
    dp = [0]*16
    dp[9] = 1
    for ch in s:
        tr = trans[ch - 48]  # if ch is int byte
        ndp = [0]*16
        # local variables for speed
        for R in range(1,16):
            cnt = dp[R]
            if cnt:
                for R2, m in tr[R]:
                    ndp[R2] += cnt * m
        for j in range(1,16):
            ndp[j] %= MOD
        dp = ndp
    ans = 0
    for R in range(1,16):
        if R & (1 | 8):  # bit0 or bit3
            ans += dp[R]
    print(ans % MOD)
```
Need if s is bytes, iterating gives ints. ch - 48 for '0' or '1'. Good. N maybe not used except read.

Potential issue: If s contains newline? split removes. Good.

for R, R2, m in updates:
    cnt = dp[R]

Now, let's think about possible bug in precomputing T masks. Let's write a small helper to generate T masks from base formula to avoid manual errors.

```
def make_T():
    T = [[0]*4 for _ in range(2)]
    for s in range(2):
        for u in range(2):
            for v in range(2):
                base = 1 + v - u
                if s == 0:
                    outs = [base]
                else:
                    outs = [base, base+1]
                for a in outs:
                    if 0 <= a <= 3:
                        T[s][a] |= 1 << ((u << 1) | v)
    return T
```
This is less error-prone and still tiny. Let's use this in code. It computes T[0][1] includes (0,0),(1,1); T[0][2] (0,1); T[0][0] (1,0); T[0][3] none. For s=1, includes all. Good.

Then compose function.

Could precompute `comp` 16x16:
```
comp = [[0]*16 for _ in range(16)]
for R in range(16):
    for Tmask in range(16):
        res=0
        for u in range(2):
            for v in range(2):
                if R & (1 << ((u<<1)|v)):
                    for w in range(2):
                        if Tmask & (1 << ((v<<1)|w)):
                            res |= 1 << ((u<<1)|w)
        comp[R][Tmask] = res
```
Then trans:
```
trans = []
for s in range(2):
    tr = [[] for _ in range(16)]
    for R in range(1,16):
        cnt = {}
        for a in range(4):
            R2 = comp[R][T[s][a]]
            if R2:
                cnt[R2] = cnt.get(R2,0)+1
        tr[R] = list(cnt.items())
    trans.append(tr)
```
Good.

Potential final code:

```
import sys

MOD = 998244353

def build():
    # T[s][a]
    T = [[0]*4 for _ in range(2)]
    for s in range(2):
        for u in range(2):
            for v in range(2):
                base = 1 + v - u
                if s == 0:
                    outs = (base,)
                else:
                    outs = (base, base+1)
                for a in outs:
                    if 0 <= a <= 3:
                        T[s][a] |= 1 << ((u << 1) | v)
    comp = [[0]*16 for _ in range(16)]
    for R in range(16):
        for Tm in range(16):
            res = 0
            for u in range(2):
                for v in range(2):
                    if R & (1 << ((u << 1) | v)):
                        for w in range(2):
                            if Tm & (1 << ((v << 1) | w)):
                                res |= 1 << ((u << 1) | w)
            comp[R][Tm] = res
    trans = []
    for s in range(2):
        tr = [[] for _ in range(16)]
        for R in range(1, 16):
            cnt = {}
            for a in range(4):
                R2 = comp[R][T[s][a]]
                if R2:
                    cnt[R2] = cnt.get(R2, 0) + 1
            tr[R] = list(cnt.items())
        trans.append(tr)
    return trans

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    if not data:
        return
    N = int(data[0])
    s = data[1]
    trans = build()
    # reachable states
    reachable = [False]*16
    reachable[9] = True
    stack = [9]
    while stack:
        R = stack.pop()
        for si in range(2):
            for R2, _ in trans[si][R]:
                if not reachable[R2]:
                    reachable[R2] = True
                    stack.append(R2)
    active = [R for R in range(1,16) if reachable[R]]
    dp = [0]*16
    dp[9] = 1
    mod = MOD
    for ch in s:
        tr = trans[ch - 48]
        ndp = [0]*16
        for R in active:
            cnt = dp[R]
            if cnt:
                for R2, m in tr[R]:
                    ndp[R2] += cnt * m
        dp = [x % mod for x in ndp]
    ans = 0
    for R in active:
        if R & 9:  # bits 0 or 3? 9 binary 1001, yes bit0 and bit3. Wait bit3 value 8, bit0 value1, so 9. 
            ans += dp[R]
    print(ans % mod)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Average s=1 maybe 3.3. Overall ~3 updates per state. 12*3=36 updates per position, 36M updates. Outer 12M, mod 16M. Total ~64M operations. Should be okay in PyPy maybe around 3-4 seconds. AtCoder Python time maybe 2 sec? Need maybe optimize more.

Can we reduce mod operations? Instead of modding all 16 every position, we can let dp values grow and mod every e.g. 8 positions? But dp values would grow exponentially by factor 4^8 ~65536 each position? Wait if we don't mod for L positions, starting from <MOD, after each position values multiply by up to 4 per state and sum; after L positions values ~MOD * (4^L) maybe huge (for L=8, 1e9*65536=6e13, still okay; for L=16, 4e18, Python big ints slower). We could mod every few positions to reduce mod operations. But dp values also grow due to merging; maximum count of distinct prefixes length t is 4^t, so after L positions ~4^L. If L=8, ~65536*MOD=6e13, fits in 64-bit (2^46), Python int still one/two limbs. If L=16, ~4e18 > 2^62, still maybe okay but bigger. We can mod every 8 or 16 positions to reduce 16M mods to 2M mods. But then inner additions use larger cnt, product cnt*m up to 2e14, ndp sum up to 36*2e14=7e15, still fits in 64-bit? 7e15 < 9e18, yes. If mod every 16, counts could be ~4^16*MOD ≈ 4.3e18? Actually 4^16=4.29e9, times 1e9 =4.3e18, within 64-bit signed 9.22e18. Sum over 36 terms maybe 1.5e20, exceeding 64-bit but Python big ints (3 limbs) still okay. But modulo every 16 reduces operations. However, if we don't mod, dp values represent actual counts modulo? Wait if we delay modulo, we are computing exact counts of distinct prefixes, which can be huge (4^t). For t=1e6 impossible. But we can delay for a fixed L and then reduce; because recurrence linear over integers, taking modulo every L positions is valid. We can accumulate exact counts for L steps, but exact counts grow exponentially; for L=16, max count 4^16 ≈ 4e9 per state? Actually total distinct strings length 16 is 4^16=4.29e9, so dp per state ≤4.29e9, not times MOD. Wait dp values are counts of distinct prefixes, not modulo. If we start with dp[9]=1 and don't mod, after t positions dp values are exact counts of strings leading to each state, total ≤4^t. For t=16, total 4.29e9, per state ≤4.29e9, product with m ≤1.7e10, ndp sum ≤36*1.7e10=6e11. Actually if we delay modulo from the start, counts are exact and much smaller than MOD for t<15 (4^15=1e9). For t=20, 4^20=1.1e12. If we mod every L positions, dp values are modulo after each mod, but between mods they grow from <MOD to at most <MOD * 4^L? Wait after mod, dp values are residues <MOD. Then next L steps without mod compute exact counts modulo? No, if we start with residues instead of exact counts, the recurrence over integers with residues yields values that are congruent modulo MOD to true counts, but not equal to exact counts. They can grow by factor 4 each step from residues, so after L steps values up to MOD*4^L, as I said. If L=8, ~6e13; if L=16, ~4e18. This is okay. But if L=20, ~1e21, bigger but Python can handle. We can choose L=8 or 16 to reduce mod operations. But note if we start with residues, the values are not exact counts but linear combinations; modulo at end of block gives correct residues. This is standard. We can implement block modulo to reduce 16M mods to e.g. 1M.

But is it necessary? Maybe not. But it can improve speed. However, if we delay modulo, inner additions use larger integers, which may slow down. Need balance. Mod operation 16M is not huge; but big int additions with 64-bit vs 30-bit maybe similar. Let's estimate: If mod every position, cnt <1e9 (30 bits), product <4e9 (33 bits), ndp sum <1e11 (37 bits), all fit in 64-bit? Python int uses 30-bit limbs, 37 bits =2 limbs. If mod every 8, cnt <1e9*4^8=6.5e13 (46 bits), product <2.6e14 (48 bits), ndp sum <1e16 (54 bits), 2 limbs. Still okay. Let's maybe refine code for speed:

```
def main():
    import sys
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    if not data: return
    s = data[1]
    trans = build_trans()
    trans0, trans1 = trans
    # BFS active
    reachable = [False]*16
    reachable[9] = True
    stack = [9]
    while stack:
        R = stack.pop()
        for tr in (trans0, trans1):
            for R2, _ in tr[R]:
                if not reachable[R2]:
                    reachable[R2] = True
                    stack.append(R2)
    active0 = [(R, trans0[R]) for R in range(1,16) if reachable[R]]
    active1 = [(R, trans1[R]) for R in range(1,16) if reachable[R]]
    dp = [0]*16
    dp[9] = 1
    mod = MOD
    pos = 0
    for ch in s:
        info = active0 if ch == 48 else active1
        ndp = [0]*16
        for R, trR in info:
            cnt = dp[R]
            if cnt:
                for R2, m in trR:
                    ndp[R2] += cnt * m
        dp = ndp
        pos += 1
        if pos == 8:
            dp = [x % mod for x in dp]
            pos = 0
    if pos:
        dp = [x % mod for x in dp]
    ans = 0
    for R, _ in active0:  # active0 and active1 same states
        if R & 9:
            ans += dp[R]
    print(ans % mod)
```
Note active0 and active1 have same R list but different transitions. We can just use `active_states = [R ...]` and `active0`/`active1` as lists of (R, trans). For answer, iterate `active_states`.

```
active_states = [R for R in range(1,16) if reachable[R]]
active0 = [(R, trans0[R]) for R in active_states]
active1 = [(R, trans1[R]) for R in active_states]
...
for R in active_states:
    if R & 9: ans += dp[R]
```
Good.

Let's maybe write build_trans more compactly:

```
def build_trans():
    T = [[0]*4 for _ in range(2)]
    for s in range(2):
        for u in range(2):
            for v in range(2):
                base = 1 + v - u
                for a in (base, base+1) if s else (base,):
                    T[s][a] |= 1 << ((u << 1) | v)
    comp = [[0]*16 for _ in range(16)]
    for R in range(16):
        for Tm in range(16):
            res = 0
            for u in range(2):
                for v in range(2):
                    if R >> ((u << 1) | v) & 1:
                        for w in range(2):
                            if Tm >> ((v << 1) | w) & 1:
                                res |= 1 << ((u << 1) | w)
            comp[R][Tm] = res
    trans = []
    for s in range(2):
        tr = [[] for _ in range(16)]
        for R in range(1,16):
            cnt = {}
            for a in range(4):
                R2 = comp[R][T[s][a]]
                if R2:
                    cnt[R2] = cnt.get(R2, 0) + 1
            tr[R] = list(cnt.items())
        trans.append(tr)
    return trans
```
This is fine. Note `(base, base+1) if s else (base,)` creates tuple; okay.

Could use `range(2)` loops in compose; fine.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the fact that the code's `dp` values after block mod

=== ANSWER ===
.</think>```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353


def build_trans():
    # T[s][a] = bitmask of transitions (prev_state, next_state)
    # bit index: (prev << 1) | next
    T = [[0] * 4 for _ in range(2)]

    for s in range(2):
        for u in range(2):
            for v in range(2):
                base = 1 + v - u
                outs = (base, base + 1) if s else (base,)
                for a in outs:
                    T[s][a] |= 1 << ((u << 1) | v)

    # comp[R][Tm] = relation composition R ∘ Tm
    comp = [[0] * 16 for _ in range(16)]
    for R in range(16):
        for Tm in range(16):
            res = 0
            for u in range(2):
                for v in range(2):
                    if (R >> ((u << 1) | v)) & 1:
                        for w in range(2):
                            if (Tm >> ((v << 1) | w)) & 1:
                                res |= 1 << ((u << 1) | w)
            comp[R][Tm] = res

    trans = []
    for s in range(2):
        tr = [[] for _ in range(16)]
        for R in range(1, 16):
            cnt = {}
            for a in range(4):
                R2 = comp[R][T[s][a]]
                if R2:
                    cnt[R2] = cnt.get(R2, 0) + 1
            tr[R] = list(cnt.items())
        trans.append(tr)

    return trans


def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    if not data:
        return

    N = int(data[0])
    s = data[1]

    trans0, trans1 = build_trans()

    # Reachable non-empty relation states from the identity relation.
    reachable = [False] * 16
    reachable[9] = True
    stack = [9]
    while stack:
        R = stack.pop()
        for tr in (trans0, trans1):
            for R2, _ in tr[R]:
                if not reachable[R2]:
                    reachable[R2] = True
                    stack.append(R2)

    active_states = [R for R in range(1, 16) if reachable[R]]
    active0 = [(R, trans0[R]) for R in active_states]
    active1 = [(R, trans1[R]) for R in active_states]

    dp = [0] * 16
    dp[9] = 1

    mod = MOD
    pos = 0

    for ch in s:
        info = active0 if ch == 48 else active1
        ndp = [0] * 16

        for R, trR in info:
            cnt = dp[R]
            if cnt:
                for R2, m in trR:
                    ndp[R2] += cnt * m

        dp = ndp
        pos += 1
        if pos == 8:
            dp = [x % mod for x in dp]
            pos = 0

    if pos:
        dp = [x % mod for x in dp]

    ans = 0
    for R in active_states:
        if R & 9:  # diagonal pair (0,0) or (1,1)
            ans += dp[R]

    print(ans % mod)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
